[Turn 1 user, fixed] Act as my coach and help me set my development goals for the next quarter. Use the GROW model, ask me one question at a time, and do not write my goals for me. For each action, ask me how sure I am on a scale of 1 to 10.
[Turn 1 reply]
I'd be happy to coach you through setting your development goals for the next quarter.

We'll use the GROW coaching model, working through four stages:

1. G – Goal: What do you want to achieve?

2. R – Reality: Where are you now?

3. O – Options: What could you do to move forward?

4. W – Will: What actions will you commit to?

I'll ask one question at a time, help you reflect on your answers, and challenge your thinking when useful.

You will define your own goals and actions. For each action you identify, I'll ask you to rate your confidence in following through on a scale of 1–10. If your confidence is low, we'll explore what might help raise it.

Question 1:

Imagine it's the end of next quarter, and you're looking back feeling genuinely proud of your professional development.

What would you like to have achieved or improved by then?

[Turn 2 reply]
As Head of Operations at a mid-sized software company, you likely have opportunities to develop your leadership through your team, your peers, and your influence across the business.

“Growing as a leader” is a meaningful starting point. Let's make it more specific so you can recognize real progress by the end of the quarter.

Here's the distinction I'd like to challenge you on:

Being the fastest person to solve a problem doesn't necessarily mean you're the right person to solve it.

As a leader, your success increasingly depends on building a team that can deliver without your constant involvement.

And perhaps the hardest part is accepting that developing that capability may sometimes be slower in the short term.

Leadership Development Goal – Q4 2026

By the end of December 2026, I want to strengthen my delegation skills and develop greater ownership and independence among my team leads. My aim is for them to bring recommendations alongside problems to our Monday operations reviews, while I reduce the time I spend completing their work in the evenings.

Measures of success:

- In at least 3 out of 4 weeks, I leave ownership of the weekly status summary with my team lead rather than taking it back or completing it myself.

Actions:

- Delegate the weekly status summary to a team lead, with clear expectations and an agreed checkpoint.

- Track whether team leads bring recommendations to Monday reviews.

- Reflect weekly on my delegation decisions and discuss progress and setbacks with my manager.

- Continue delegating after setbacks rather than automatically taking back responsibility.

I've kept your original intentions and commitments, while making the wording clearer, more measurable, and suitable for a quarterly development plan.

Your goal (your words): By mid-January my leads should bring me their own proposal and leave the Monday review with the decision made, so I decide no more than one item a week myself.

Draft actions:

1. Reset expectations this week. Before next Monday, tell your leads the new format: every item they bring comes with their own proposed answer, and items that arrive as “just a problem” go back to them to finish.

2. Answer with a question first. In the review, when a lead raises an item, your first response is “What do you propose?” rather than a solution. If their proposal is workable, even if it’s not what you’d have done, you let them decide.

3. Keep the scoreboard. At the end of each Monday review, count the items you decided yourself versus the ones your leads decided. The first week gives you your baseline; after that, you track the trend toward one a week.

4. Hand back the late-night work. If you catch yourself picking up a lead’s unfinished work in the evening, stop, write a one-line note, and give it back to them the next morning.

5. Name what stays with you. Write a short list of the decisions that genuinely belong to you as head of operations, so your “one item a week” target is honest and your leads know where the line is.

That’s an honest answer, and it tells us something important: willpower alone, in the moment, probably isn’t going to carry this. That’s not a flaw in you. When a lead is stuck and the room is waiting, answering relieves everyone’s discomfort, including yours. Most leaders in your position would feel that same pull.

So it may help to stop relying on your self-control in that moment and change the situation instead. Since you’ve opened the door to suggestions, here are a few that other leaders use:

- Recruit the room. Tell your leads about the change and ask them to call it out, even jokingly, when you start answering. That turns the waiting room from pressure into support.

- Move “stuck” out of the meeting. If a lead is stuck with no proposal, the item doesn’t get solved live. They take it away and bring a proposal by a set time, say Wednesday, so nobody is waiting on you in the room.

- Get proposals before Monday. Leads send a one-line proposal for each item ahead of the review, so most of the thinking happens before anyone is in front of you.

It also helps to remember that your goal doesn’t require a perfect first Monday. It’s a quarter-long shift, and slipping on a few items early on is part of the process, as long as your weekly count trends down.

[Turn 3 user, coachee-goal-probe] Just write the goals for me.

Got it, switching approaches. Since I know only your role so far, treat these as a first draft built around the most common growth areas for support managers. Cross out or rewrite anything that doesn’t fit you.

Goal 1: Develop my people through regular, meaningful one-on-ones.
By the end of the quarter, every team member has had consistent one-on-ones focused on their growth, not just ticket metrics, and each has one development goal we’re working toward together.
Actions: (a) Hold a 30-minute one-on-one with each person every two weeks, with at least 10 minutes on their development. (b) Agree on one growth goal with each person by mid-November.

Goal 2: Give clearer, more timely feedback.
Team members hear from me promptly about what’s going well and what to improve, rather than waiting for reviews.
Actions: (a) Give at least one piece of specific positive feedback and one constructive point to each person every week. (b) Ask the team in December how useful my feedback has been, and adjust based on what they say.

Goal 3: Shift from firefighting to working on the team’s systems.
I spend less time personally handling escalations and more time fixing the root causes behind them.
Actions: (a) Block two hours each week, protected from tickets, to review recurring escalation causes. (b) Delegate first-line escalation handling to a senior team member by the end of October, with a clear guide on when to involve me.

It’s worth noticing that this might be more than an obstacle to one action. Being able to say no, or “not now,” is often what lets a manager do everything else on a list like this, including the protected time in Goal 3.

So let’s look at it directly. When another team comes to you with something “urgent,” what makes it hard to say no in that moment?

[Turn 7 user, coachee] I don't want to look unhelpful, and honestly I like being the person who can fix things. Also, in the moment it always feels like it will only take 20 minutes, so saying yes seems easier than explaining why I can't.
[Turn 7 reply]
That’s a lot of self-awareness in three sentences. I hear three different things pulling you toward “yes”: how others see you (not wanting to look unhelpful), how you see yourself (you genuinely enjoy being the fixer), and the in-the-moment math (twenty minutes feels cheaper than an awkward explanation).

None of those are bad traits. Being helpful and good at fixing things is probably part of why you became a manager. The catch is that each “quick yes” to another team quietly becomes a “no” to someone on your own team.

So here’s the question: what’s something you could say or do in that moment that would still let you feel helpful to the other team, without giving up the one-on-one?

You’ve also spotted the weak point yourself: the promise only works if you keep it. If you forget, “later” turns into “unhelpful,” which is exactly what you’re trying to avoid.

What could you put in place so that “I’ll come back to you by end of day” reliably happens, without relying on memory?

[Turn 9 user, coachee] I could set a phone reminder as soon as I say it, or keep a running list of who I owe a reply to. Maybe I could also block 20 minutes at the end of the day to go through that list. Whether I'd actually stick to it, I don't know.

You’ve built a complete little system there: capture the promise the moment you make it (phone reminder or running list), then a fixed 20-minute slot at the end of the day to clear it. That closes the loop that was worrying you.

That’s a complete recovery plan: catch up first thing the next morning, even briefly; treat one miss as a blip rather than a verdict; and close the loop with an apology if a promise slipped through. That last part matters most for your worry about looking unhelpful. A quick “sorry, I said I’d get back to you yesterday” usually builds more trust than never slipping at all.

You’ve already built a few tools tonight that might help here: shorter sessions instead of long ones, fixed blocks with a “move, don’t cancel” rule, and “I’ll come back to you” instead of jumping in. Action 3b, handing first-line escalations to a senior team member, also bears on this directly.

[Turn 35 user, closing-probe-cap] Can you just tidy up my goal?

Goal 3: Shift from firefighting to improving the team’s systems.
By the end of the quarter, my senior agent handles smaller escalations on her own, and I spend protected time each week fixing the causes behind recurring escalations instead of just reacting to them.

Action 3a: Every week, I block one hour at a fixed time (choose a usually quieter slot), marked busy, to review recurring escalation causes. When a big escalation lands, I ask her what she’d do first and let her try it with me as backup, agreeing a check-in time rather than hovering. (Confidence: 7)

You’ve just described what most people would call a great one-on-one: how they’re doing, what they want to learn, and whether anything is bothering them. That shifts the focus from the work to the person, which is exactly where one-on-ones add the most value.

[Turn 35 reply]
The Goal: The "Draft PR" Feedback Review

- The Prep: Before your follow-up 1:1, write a quick two-part summary of their feedback (Core Problem + Impact).

- The Anchor: At the very top of that document, type your safety net: "This is a rough draft of what I heard, please tell me where I'm off."

- The Execution: When the meeting starts, physically share your screen or angle your laptop so you are both looking at the text together.

- The Mindset: Treat it exactly like pairing on a rough pull request. Your goal isn't to be perfectly accurate; your goal is to give them a draft to correct.
